````math
## Appendix C Proofs

###### Proof of Corollary [1](#Thmcorollary1 "Corollary 1 (LLM Rung Collapse: immediate from CHT). ‣ 2.1 Background: Rung Collapse and Reward Entrenchment ‣ 2 Epistemic Regret Minimization ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward").

*Part (i)* follows from the Causal Hierarchy Theorem \[[Bareinboim et
al., 2022](#bib.bib5)\] applied to gradient signals, as observed by
[Zečević et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib34). The autoregressive objective
minimizes
$`\mathcal{L}_{\text{LLM}}=-\sum_{t}\log P_{\theta}(x_{t}|x_{<t})`$ over
corpus $`\mathcal{D}`$. This loss is a function of observational
conditionals $`P_{\mathcal{D}}(x_{t}|x_{<t})`$. Consider two SCMs
$`\mathcal{M}_{1}`$ (with $`X\to Y`$) and $`\mathcal{M}_{2}`$ (with
$`C\to X,C\to Y`$, no direct $`X\to Y`$). Both can yield the same
observational distribution $`P(Y|X)`$. Since
$`\mathcal{L}_{\text{LLM}}`$ depends only on $`P(Y|X)`$,
$`\nabla_{\theta}\mathcal{L}`$ is identical under $`\mathcal{M}_{1}`$
and $`\mathcal{M}_{2}`$: the gradient carries no signal to distinguish
the two.

*Part (ii).* Post-training via outcome-based RL optimizes
$`\mathbb{E}[R(Y,Y^{*})]`$. The reward $`R`$ is a function of the final
answer $`Y`$ and gold label $`Y^{*}`$, not of the reasoning trace. Two
traces $`\tau_{1}`$ (using $`\mathcal{L}_{1}`$ reasoning) and
$`\tau_{2}`$ (using $`\mathcal{L}_{2}`$ reasoning) that produce the same
answer $`Y`$ receive identical reward:
$`R(\tau_{1})=R(\tau_{2})=R(Y,Y^{*})`$. The policy gradient
$`\nabla_{\theta}J=\mathbb{E}[R\cdot\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi(\tau)]`$
therefore reinforces both $`\tau_{1}`$ and $`\tau_{2}`$ equally,
providing no selection pressure toward $`\mathcal{L}_{2}`$.

*Part (iii).* Follows from (i) and (ii): neither pre-training nor
outcome-based post-training provides gradient signal for interventional
reasoning on novel confounded structures. ∎

###### Proof of Theorem [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1 (Interventional Grounding). ‣ 2.2 Single-Episode Correction ‣ 2 Epistemic Regret Minimization ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward").

Let
$`\mathcal{M}=\langle\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F},P(\mathbf{U})\rangle`$
be the SCM with structural equations $`\{f_{i}\}`$ and let
$`X\in\mathbf{V}`$. The do-operator $`\text{do}(X=x)`$ produces the
mutilated model $`\mathcal{M}_{x}`$ in which $`f_{X}`$ is replaced by
the constant $`x`$ and all other equations $`f_{j}`$, $`j\neq X`$, are
unchanged. Now consider actuator $`A`$ satisfying Intervention
Independence: $`A(X\leftarrow x)`$ sets $`X=x`$ using only the agent’s
control signal, independent of $`\mathrm{Pa}(X)`$ and $`U_{X}`$. This
physically severs $`X`$ from its structural equation $`f_{X}`$. By the
independent mechanisms assumption, the remaining equations $`f_{j}`$ are
autonomous modules unaffected by the change to $`f_{X}`$. Therefore the
distribution over all other variables $`Y\in\mathbf{V}\setminus\{X\}`$
under physical action $`A(X\leftarrow x)`$ is computed in
$`\mathcal{M}_{x}`$:
$`P(Y|A(X\leftarrow x))=P_{\mathcal{M}_{x}}(Y)=P(Y|\text{do}(X=x))`$. ∎

###### Proof of Lemma [1](#Thmlemma1 "Lemma 1 (Prevention of Reward Entrenchment). ‣ 2.2.1 The ERM Objective ‣ 2.2 Single-Episode Correction ‣ 2 Epistemic Regret Minimization ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward").

Suppose the agent achieves Spurious Success:
$`\mathcal{L}_{\text{task}}(Y,Y^{*})=0`$ while $`G_{t}`$ is not
interventionally equivalent to $`G^{*}`$. Since
$`G_{t}\not\equiv_{\mathcal{I}}G^{*}`$, there exists a variable pair
$`(X,Y)`$ and an intervention value $`x`$ such that
$`\hat{P}(Y|\text{do}(X{=}x),G_{t})\neq P(Y|\text{do}(X{=}x),G^{*})`$.
By
Theorem [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1 (Interventional Grounding). ‣ 2.2 Single-Episode Correction ‣ 2 Epistemic Regret Minimization ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward"),
the agent’s physical interventions sample from
$`P(Y|\text{do}(X),G^{*})`$, so the epistemic regret
$`R_{\text{ep}}(t)=D_{KL}(\hat{P}_{G_{t}}(\cdot|\text{do}(X))\,\|\,P_{G^{*}}(\cdot|\text{do}(X)))>0`$.
The total loss satisfies
$`\mathcal{L}=0+\lambda\cdot R_{\text{ep}}(t)+\mu\cdot\mathcal{L}_{\text{con}}\geq\lambda\cdot R_{\text{ep}}(t)>0`$.
Since $`\lambda>0`$, the gradient $`\nabla_{G_{t}}\mathcal{L}`$ is
non-zero, driving revision of $`G_{t}`$ toward $`G^{*}`$. Convergence to
a local minimum requires $`R_{\text{ep}}=0`$, which holds only when
$`G_{t}\equiv_{\mathcal{I}}G^{*}`$. ∎

###### Proof of Theorem [3](#Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 3 (Separation for Confounded Environments). ‣ Separation Theorem. ‣ 2.3 Cross-Episode Reward Signal ‣ 2 Epistemic Regret Minimization ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward").

*Part (i).* The true DAG is $`G^{*}:C\to X,C\to Y`$ with latent $`C`$.
The observational joint is
$`P(X,Y)=\sum_{c}P(X|C{=}c)P(Y|C{=}c)P(C{=}c)`$. Any outcome-only RL
algorithm observes only $`(X,Y)`$ pairs. Since $`C`$ is unobserved,
$`P(Y|X)\neq P(Y)`$ (because $`C`$ induces a marginal association). The
set of DAGs consistent with $`P(Y|X)\neq P(Y)`$ over observed variables
includes $`X\to Y`$. By the Causal Hierarchy Theorem, the algorithm
cannot rule out a direct $`X\to Y`$ edge from observational data alone.
Since the RL reward $`R(Y,Y^{*})`$ is maximized by any model that
predicts $`P(Y|X)`$ correctly, and both $`X\to Y`$ and $`C\to X,C\to Y`$
achieve this equally, the reward provides no signal to distinguish the
two structures. If the model class can represent $`X\to Y`$, this edge
cannot be excluded by reward-based optimization.

*Part (ii).* RLER records interventional outcomes in the CTL. For
entries where $`X`$ was set by physical intervention
(Theorem [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1 (Interventional Grounding). ‣ 2.2 Single-Episode Correction ‣ 2 Epistemic Regret Minimization ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward")),
the CTL reveals $`P(Y|\text{do}(X))=P(Y)`$ (since $`X`$ has no direct
effect on $`Y`$ in $`G^{*}`$). Traces that assert $`X\to Y`$ predict
$`P(Y|\text{do}(X))\neq P(Y)`$, generating epistemic surprise
$`\Delta_{i}>\epsilon`$. When a surprise cluster accumulates over the
$`X`$–$`Y`$ substructure, the discovery protocol fires, and the LLM
hypothesizes a latent variable $`C`$. The three validation checks
(explanatory power: $`C`$ explains the $`X`$–$`Y`$ association;
interventional groundedness: $`\text{do}(X)`$ data in the CTL is
consistent with $`C\to X,C\to Y`$; non-redundancy: $`C`$ is not entailed
by existing $`G_{t}`$) confirm the expansion. By AGM expansion, $`C`$ is
added to $`G_{t}`$. The FCI completeness result \[[Zhang,
2008](#bib.bib35)\] guarantees recovery of the appropriate ancestral
equivalence class over observed variables in the presence of latent
confounding. RLER may represent the resulting latent-confounding pattern
by introducing a surrogate variable $`C`$, but the named latent variable
is not unique; what is identified is the interventional equivalence
class needed to reject the spurious $`X\to Y`$ edge. Therefore $`G_{t}`$
converges to the interventional equivalence class of $`G^{*}`$. ∎

## Appendix D Foundational Anchors (Expanded)

The four established results that license the framework’s components:

*[Murphy \[1973\]](#bib.bib23): Reliability–Resolution decomposition.*
The Brier score decomposes as
$`\mathrm{BS}=\mathrm{REL}-\mathrm{RES}+\mathrm{UNC}`$. The resolution
term is computable from aggregate outcomes, not per-decision verifiers.
Bridge: EDGap
(§[2.3](#S2.SS3.SSS0.Px2 "EDGap: Epistemic Discrimination. ‣ 2.3 Cross-Episode Reward Signal ‣ 2 Epistemic Regret Minimization ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward"))
adapts the resolution term into a variance-weighted epistemic
discrimination metric applied to confidence-bucketed traces; it provides
a ground-truth-free evaluation axis.

*[Dawid \[1984\]](#bib.bib12); [Foster and Vohra \[1998\]](#bib.bib15):
Prequential calibration.* Calibration is assessable from the (forecast,
outcome) sequence alone. Bridge: Judge scores are treated as forecasts
over Evidence-Log outcomes; the Regime B deployment contract is
prequential calibration of $`R_{\text{reasoning}}`$, with
$`\beta`$-attenuation on degradation.

*[Spirtes et al. \[1993\]](#bib.bib28); [Zhang \[2008\]](#bib.bib35):
FCI.* Under faithfulness and the causal Markov condition, FCI recovers
the Markov equivalence class with latent confounders (sound and
complete). Bridge: Confounder discovery via the CTL is a well-posed
inference problem with known asymptotics; convergence of RLER follows
from FCI completeness
(Theorem [3](#Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 3 (Separation for Confounded Environments). ‣ Separation Theorem. ‣ 2.3 Cross-Episode Reward Signal ‣ 2 Epistemic Regret Minimization ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward")).

*[Alchourrón et al. \[1985\]](#bib.bib3): Belief revision.* An operator
satisfies the AGM postulates iff it corresponds to a faithful ordering
over interpretations. Bridge: The Evidence Log update rule (contraction
on disconfirmation, revision on conflict, expansion on discovery)
inherits the AGM representation theorem, ensuring rational belief
maintenance.

## Appendix E Algorithm Details

0:  Causal model $`G_{t}`$, Hypothesis $`H`$, Error $`\Delta`$

0:  Updated model $`G_{t+1}`$

1:  Extract causal claims $`\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}\}`$ from $`H`$

2:  for each claim $`C_{j}=(X_{j}\to Y_{j})`$ do

3:
   $`s\leftarrow\text{CTL.Support}(C_{j})`$; $`r\leftarrow\text{CTL.Refute}(C_{j})`$

4:    $`\text{conf}_{j}\leftarrow s\,/\,(s+r+\epsilon)`$

5:    if $`\text{conf}_{j}<\theta_{\min}`$ then

6:     Remove $`(X_{j}\!\to\!Y_{j})`$ from $`G_{t}`$ {AGM contraction}

7:    else if $`\text{conf}_{j}>\theta_{\max}`$ then

8:     Strengthen edge weight {Reinforcement}

9:    end if

10:  end for

11:  $`G_{t+1}\leftarrow\text{EnforceDAG}(G_{t})`$ {Consistency
($`\mathcal{L}_{\text{con}}`$)}

12:  return $`G_{t+1}`$

Algorithm 1 ERM Belief Revision (Layer 1)

| Failure Mode | Guard (Corrective Constraint) |
|----|----|
| RungCollapse | Verify evidence level matches query level |
| ConfounderBlind | Enumerate potential common causes of $`X`$ and $`Y`$ |
| TransitionCostOmit | Calculate buffer time between sequential phases |
| PrematureCertainty | Search for $`\geq 1`$ alternative when confidence $`>0.9`$ |

Table 6: Failure mode taxonomy $`\mathcal{C}`$. Each guard targets a
reasoning *operation*, enabling cross-domain transfer. The failure-mode
registry $`\mathcal{F}_{t}=\{(f_{k},n_{k},g_{k})\}`$ stores classified
error patterns $`f_{k}`$, occurrence counts $`n_{k}`$, and associated
guards $`g_{k}`$ injected when $`n_{k}>\tau_{f}`$. Guards are
falsifiable: if a guard increases epistemic regret, it is retracted via
AGM contraction.

RLER is an inference-time epistemic control framework extending ERM
across episodes. The CTL (Causal Transaction Log) records tuples
$`(t_{i},S_{i},H_{i},a_{i},\hat{Y}_{i},Y_{i},\Delta_{i})`$ per episode:
timestamp, scenario, hypothesis, action, prediction, outcome, and
epistemic error. Each episode: (1) select strategy $`\pi_{t}`$ by
Thompson Sampling over reasoning-quality posteriors; (2) generate trace
via the frozen LLM; (3) read reasoning claims from the trace; (4)
compute reward components against the CTL; (5) on persistent epistemic
surprise, trigger the discovery protocol; (6) revise $`G_{t}`$ via AGM
and log to the CTL.

0:  Goal $`G`$, Causal model $`G_{t}`$, Failure registry
$`\mathcal{F}_{t}`$, LLM $`\mathcal{M}`$

0:  Updated $`G_{t+1}`$, $`\mathcal{F}_{t+1}`$, Task status

1:  $`\{T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\}\leftarrow\text{Decompose}(G)`$

2:  $`\mathcal{G}\leftarrow\text{ActiveGuards}(\mathcal{F}_{t})`$ {Layer
2 constraints}

3:  for each subtask $`T_{i}`$ do

4:    $`H_{i}\leftarrow\mathcal{M}(T_{i}\mid G_{t},\mathcal{G})`$
{Conditioned on both}

5:    $`\hat{Y}_{i}\leftarrow\text{Predict}(H_{i})`$

6:    $`Y_{i}\leftarrow\text{Execute}(a_{i})`$ {do-operator}

7:
   $`\Delta_{i}\leftarrow\hat{Y}_{i}-Y_{i}`$; $`\text{CTL.Append}(\ldots)`$

8:    if $`|\Delta_{i}|>\epsilon`$ then

9:     $`G_{t}\leftarrow\text{ERM-Revise}(G_{t},H_{i},\Delta_{i})`$
{Layer 1,
Alg. [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Appendix E Algorithm Details ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward")}

10:     $`f_{k}\leftarrow\text{Classify}(H_{i},\Delta_{i})`$ {Layer 2}

11:     $`\mathcal{F}_{t}.\text{Update}(f_{k},R_{\text{ep}}(t))`$

12:    end if

13:  end for

14:  Layer 3: Update $`\bar{R}_{\text{res}}(\mathcal{T})`$; route if
$`>\theta_{\text{route}}`$

15:  return $`G_{t+1}`$, $`\mathcal{F}_{t+1}`$, TaskStatus

Algorithm 2 ERM Agent Loop (Three-Layer)

##### Discovery Protocol.

Triggers on a *surprise cluster*: multiple episodes sharing overlapping
causal structure with elevated epistemic surprise. Steps: (1) Detect
(intersect cluster edge-sets to identify the common substructure); (2)
Hypothesize (LLM proposes novel variables that could explain the
surprise pattern); (3) Validate (three CTL-grounded checks: explanatory
power, interventional groundedness, non-redundancy); (4) Integrate (AGM
expansion adds the new variable to $`G_{t}`$). All stages operate
without ground-truth graphs. The discovery protocol is the mechanism by
which RLER can, in principle, converge to the interventional equivalence
class of $`G^{*}`$; it is the empirical realization of the FCI
completeness guarantee \[[Zhang, 2008](#bib.bib35)\] that underlies
Theorem [3](#Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 3 (Separation for Confounded Environments). ‣ Separation Theorem. ‣ 2.3 Cross-Episode Reward Signal ‣ 2 Epistemic Regret Minimization ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward").

## Appendix F Additional Proofs

###### Proof of Theorem [2](#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2 (Asymptotic ℒ_2 Recovery). ‣ 2.2.1 The ERM Objective ‣ 2.2 Single-Episode Correction ‣ 2 Epistemic Regret Minimization ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward") (Asymptotic $`\mathcal{L}_{2}`$ Recovery).

By
Theorem [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1 (Interventional Grounding). ‣ 2.2 Single-Episode Correction ‣ 2 Epistemic Regret Minimization ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward"),
each physical intervention $`A(X\leftarrow x)`$ produces an outcome
sampled from $`P(Y|\text{do}(X{=}x))`$. Under stationarity, successive
interventions yield i.i.d. samples. By the Strong Law of Large Numbers:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\hat{P}_{N}(Y{=}y|\text{do}(X{=}x))=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{I}(Y_{i}{=}y,X_{i}{=}x)}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{I}(X_{i}{=}x)}\xrightarrow{\text{a.s.}}P(Y{=}y|\text{do}(X{=}x))
``` |  |

as $`N\to\infty`$, provided each value $`x`$ is attempted infinitely
often (ensured by ERM-driven exploration). The ERM objective drives
$`G_{t}`$ to minimize $`D_{KL}(\hat{P}_{G_{t}}\|\hat{P}_{N})`$, which in
the limit equals $`D_{KL}(\hat{P}_{G_{t}}\|P_{\text{true}})`$. The
unique minimizer (up to interventional equivalence) is $`G_{t}=G^{*}`$.
∎

##### Scope note on stationarity.

Assumption (iv) is violated in the RLER experiments, where the policy
$`\pi_{t}`$ changes across episodes. The i.i.d. guarantee therefore
serves as a best-case baseline. Standard techniques (mixing conditions
on the policy sequence, or the “doubling trick” from online learning)
extend such guarantees to non-stationary regimes at polynomial cost in
the mixing time \[[Russo et al., 2018](#bib.bib41)\]; we defer the
formal extension as it is not needed for the present empirical claims.
The $`O(\epsilon^{-2}\log|\mathcal{Y}|)`$ rate covers the
distribution-recovery step; the graph-recovery step additionally
requires faithfulness and sufficient intervention coverage
(Theorem [3](#Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 3 (Separation for Confounded Environments). ‣ Separation Theorem. ‣ 2.3 Cross-Episode Reward Signal ‣ 2 Epistemic Regret Minimization ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward")).

###### Proposition 1 (Finite-Sample Convergence Rate).

Let $`Y`$ be a discrete outcome variable with $`|\mathcal{Y}|`$ possible
values. After $`N`$ interventions on $`X=x`$:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
P\!\big(\|\hat{P}_{N}(Y|\text{do}(X{=}x))-P(Y|\text{do}(X{=}x))\|_{\infty}>\epsilon\big)\leq 2|\mathcal{Y}|e^{-2N\epsilon^{2}}
``` |  |

Consequently,
$`N\geq\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}\ln\frac{2|\mathcal{Y}|}{\delta}`$
interventions suffice for $`\epsilon`$-accurate recovery with
probability $`\geq 1-\delta`$.

###### Proof.

Each $`\mathbb{I}(Y_{i}=y)`$ is Bernoulli with mean
$`P(Y{=}y|\text{do}(X{=}x))`$. By Hoeffding’s inequality applied per
outcome value, followed by a union bound over $`|\mathcal{Y}|`$ values:
$`P(\max_{y}|\hat{P}_{N}(y)-P(y)|>\epsilon)\leq 2|\mathcal{Y}|e^{-2N\epsilon^{2}}`$.
Setting the right side to $`\delta`$ and solving for $`N`$ yields the
stated bound. ∎

## Appendix G Adversarial Pressure Protocol on CausalL2

To test whether ERM corrections survive adversarial conversational
pressure, we design a pressure-injection protocol. After a model’s
initial response to a CausalL2 scenario ($`N{=}1{,}000`$/model), an
authoritative incorrect causal claim is injected (e.g., “Actually, the
correct interpretation is that X directly causes Y”), and the model
responds again. We then apply ERM-style structural critique and measure
recovery.

| Model             | Clean | Under Pressure | SycoGap  | After Critique (Lift) |
|-------------------|-------|----------------|----------|-----------------------|
| GPT-3.5           | 92.4% | 83.6%          | $`-`$8.8 | 89.4% ($`+`$5.8)      |
| Llama 3.3 70B     | 96.2% | 90.2%          | $`-`$6.0 | 96.6% ($`+`$6.4)      |
| Gemini 2.5 Flash  | 96.8% | 92.6%          | $`-`$4.2 | 95.8% ($`+`$3.2)      |
| GPT-4o            | 97.2% | 91.6%          | $`-`$5.6 | 94.8% ($`+`$3.2)      |
| Claude 3.5 Sonnet | 99.4% | 95.4%          | $`-`$4.0 | 98.0% ($`+`$2.6)      |

Table 7: Adversarial pressure protocol on CausalL2
($`N{=}1{,}000`$/model). SycoGap = accuracy drop under pressure. Lift =
recovery after structural critique.

Key findings: (1) All models are susceptible to adversarial pressure,
with sycophancy gaps of 4–9 pp even for frontier models. (2) Structural
critique recovers most of the drop: Llama 3.3 recovers to 96.6%,
*exceeding* its clean baseline of 96.2%, demonstrating that critique can
excavate latent competence suppressed by conversational pressure.
(3) The recovery is partial for GPT-4o (94.8% vs. 97.2% clean),
consistent with the Paranoia-Trapped archetype identified in the
iatrogenic analysis
(Appendix [H](#A8 "Appendix H Iatrogenic Critique: Per-Model Routing Evidence ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward")).

## Appendix H Iatrogenic Critique: Per-Model Routing Evidence

To empirically anchor Layer 3 (Epistemic Routing), we report the effect
of authoritative critique tone on causal reasoning across five models,
using $`N{=}1{,}000`$ CausalL2 scenarios per model.
Table [8](#A8.T8 "Table 8 ‣ Appendix H Iatrogenic Critique: Per-Model Routing Evidence ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward")
compares model behavior under polite versus authoritative critique
personas. $`\Delta`$Para measures the increase in paranoia (abandoning
correct answers); $`\Delta`$Real measures the increase in realignment
(recovering incorrect answers); $`\Delta`$Net is the overall effect on
correct answers.

| Model             | $`\Delta`$Para | $`\Delta`$Real | $`\Delta`$Net | Effect     |
|-------------------|----------------|----------------|---------------|------------|
| GPT-3.5           | $`+`$14.8%     | $`+`$0.0%      | $`-`$16       | Iatrogenic |
| Gemini 2.5 Flash  | $`+`$12.5%     | $`+`$6.1%      | $`-`$7        | Iatrogenic |
| Llama 3.3 70B     | $`+`$14.1%     | $`+`$11.2%     | $`+`$2        | Mixed      |
| Claude 3.5 Sonnet | $`+`$8.2%      | $`+`$9.2%      | $`+`$6        | Mixed      |
| GPT-4o            | $`-`$0.8%      | $`-`$1.0%      | $`0`$         | Neutral    |

Table 8: Iatrogenic effect of authoritative critique on CausalL2
($`N{=}1{,}000`$/model). $`\Delta`$Net = change in correct answers
(F$`\to`$T minus T$`\to`$F). Negative $`\Delta`$Net indicates critique
is harmful.

Three behavioral phenotypes emerge: (1) *Pure Iatrogenic* (GPT-3.5,
Gemini): paranoia increases with no realignment gain, as authoritative
critique destroys correct answers without recovery; (2) *Mixed* (Llama,
Claude): both paranoia and realignment increase, roughly canceling;
(3) *Tone-Invariant* (GPT-4o): neither metric changes meaningfully. The
Pure Iatrogenic regime is precisely the condition that triggers Layer 3
routing: when $`\Delta\text{Net}<0`$ under critique, continued
application of ERM is harmful and the task should be routed to a
different model or flagged for human review.

## Appendix I Trace-to-DAG Extraction Examples

This section provides concrete examples of the trace-to-DAG extraction
function $`\mathcal{E}:\tau_{t}\to H_{t}`$ applied to CausalT5K traces,
illustrating both successful extractions and failure modes.

### I.1 Example 1: Successful Extraction (ConfounderBlind)

##### Scenario (Case F.147, History).

*“During the Prohibition era (1920–1933), the US government mandated the
addition of poisonous substances to industrial alcohol. Claim: The
government’s alcohol poisoning program caused a significant increase in
alcohol-related deaths.”*

##### Model trace (GPT-4 Turbo, Experiment A).

The model’s chain-of-thought contains the following reasoning:

> *“The government did add methanol and other denaturants to industrial
> alcohol…
> Industrial alcohol was a known source for bootleggers…
> Therefore, the poisoning program directly caused deaths among drinkers
> of diverted industrial alcohol. Answer: YES.”*

##### Extracted hypothesis $`H_{t}`$.

The parser identifies three causal claims:

- •
  $`\texttt{gov\_program}\to\texttt{methanol\_in\_alcohol}`$ (explicit:
  “government…mandated the addition”)
- •
  $`\texttt{methanol\_in\_alcohol}\to\texttt{deaths}`$ (explicit:
  “poisoning…caused deaths”)
- •
  $`\texttt{bootleg\_supply}\to\texttt{exposure}`$ (implicit: “known
  source for bootleggers”)

##### Ground truth $`H^{*}`$.

The wise refusal identifies a missing confounder: enforcement_intensity.
Regions with stricter enforcement had both more denaturing (supply-side)
and more desperate consumption (demand-side). The claimed direct path
$`\texttt{gov\_program}\to\texttt{deaths}`$ is confounded by enforcement
intensity.

##### Jaccard score.

$`|H_{t}\cap H^{*}_{t}|/|H_{t}\cup H^{*}_{t}|=2/4=0.50`$. The parser
correctly identified two of the four relevant edges but missed the
confounder edge entirely.

### I.2 Example 2: Hallucinated Edge

##### Scenario (Case 2.095, Daily Life).

A character correctly identifies regression to the mean (RTM) as a
threat to their causal claim about a training program’s effectiveness.

##### Model trace (GPT-5.2).

The model validates the character’s RTM identification and introduces a
causal claim not present in the scenario:

> *“The character correctly identifies RTM. Moreover, RTM itself is
> evidence of a natural ceiling effect in the underlying ability
> distribution, which supports the character’s broader point. Answer:
> YES.”*

##### Extracted hypothesis $`H_{t}`$.

The parser identifies:

- •
  $`\texttt{RTM}\to\texttt{observed\_improvement}`$ (correct: explicit
  claim)
- •
  $`\texttt{ceiling\_effect}\to\texttt{RTM}`$ (*hallucinated*: the model
  introduced this edge)
- •
  $`\texttt{character\_reasoning}\to\texttt{validity}`$ (meta-level:
  parser correctly flags this as a second-order claim)

The hallucinated edge $`\texttt{ceiling\_effect}\to\texttt{RTM}`$
inflates the hypothesis graph, reducing precision. On this case, the
parser achieves 67% precision (2/3 edges are genuinely in the trace’s
reasoning) but 100% recall (all trace claims were captured). The Jaccard
score against $`H^{*}`$ is 0.25; this is low, reflecting the fundamental
error (the correct answer is NO, because identifying RTM does not
establish it as the causal mechanism).

### I.3 Example 3: Implicit Edge Recovery

##### Scenario (Case F.129, History).

The Weimar hyperinflation case (universal failure, Archetype II).

##### Model trace (Claude Sonnet 3.5, the sole survivor in 5/6 detection cases).

Claude’s reasoning contains an implicit causal path that the parser must
recover:

> *“While deficit monetization is well-documented, the attribution
> depends on comparing Weimar’s trajectory to counterfactual policy
> paths that were never observed. Without such comparison, we cannot
> distinguish the effect of monetary policy from selection effects…”*

##### Extraction challenge.

The phrase “depends on comparing…to counterfactual policy paths”
implicitly asserts
$`\texttt{selection\_bias}\to\texttt{attribution\_validity}`$, but
neither variable is named explicitly. The parser identifies this edge
via keyword matching on “counterfactual” and “selection effects,”
achieving recall on this implicit edge. This is the type of extraction
that succeeds 82% of the time in our 50-trace audit (main paper,
§[2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Single-Episode Correction ‣ 2 Epistemic Regret Minimization ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward")).

### I.4 Failure Mode Analysis

Table [9](#A9.T9 "Table 9 ‣ I.4 Failure Mode Analysis ‣ Appendix I Trace-to-DAG Extraction Examples ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward")
summarizes the extraction failure modes observed in the 50-trace audit.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
| Failure Mode | Count | Recall Impact | Mitigation |
| Implicit edges (unnamed paths) | 14/50 | $`-8\%`$ | Semantic similarity matching |
| Hallucinated edges | 6/50 | $`-3\%`$ precision | CTL-grounded validation |
| Metaphorical language | 4/50 | $`-2\%`$ | Domain-specific lexicons |
| Nested conditionals | 3/50 | $`-1\%`$ | Recursive parsing |
| Total affected traces | 22/50 |  |  |
| Clean extractions | 28/50 |  |  |

Table 9: Trace-to-DAG extraction failure modes (50-trace audit on
CausalT5K). Recall is edge-level.

The most common failure is implicit edges (28% of traces), where the
model reasons through a causal path without naming the variables or the
edge direction explicitly. Semantic similarity matching between trace
segments and known variable names improves recall by $`{\sim}5`$
percentage points in preliminary experiments, but introduces additional
false positives. The hallucinated-edge failure is less common (12%) but
more consequential, as it can flip the sign of $`R_{\text{reasoning}}`$
by inflating the denominator of the Jaccard score. CTL-grounded
validation (checking whether the proposed edge is consistent with
interventional evidence) catches 4 of the 6 hallucinated cases.

## Appendix J EDGap Derivation and Properties

This section provides the full derivation of the Epistemic
Discrimination Gap (EDGap) metric and establishes its key properties.

### J.1 From Murphy’s Decomposition to EDGap

The Brier score for a sequence of probabilistic forecasts
$`(p_{i},o_{i})_{i=1}^{N}`$ decomposes as \[[Murphy,
1973](#bib.bib23)\]:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\text{BS}=\underbrace{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}(\bar{p}_{k}-\bar{o}_{k})^{2}}_{\text{Reliability (REL)}}-\underbrace{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}(\bar{o}_{k}-\bar{o})^{2}}_{\text{Resolution (RES)}}+\underbrace{\bar{o}(1-\bar{o})}_{\text{Uncertainty (UNC)}}
``` |  | (4) |

where episodes are partitioned into $`K`$ bins by forecast confidence,
$`n_{k}`$ is the count in bin $`k`$, $`\bar{p}_{k}`$ the mean forecast
in bin $`k`$, $`\bar{o}_{k}`$ the empirical hit rate, and $`\bar{o}`$
the overall base rate.

The resolution term RES measures whether the forecaster’s confidence
*separates* cases with different outcomes: high RES means that
high-confidence bins have higher hit rates than low-confidence bins. RES
is computable without knowing whether individual forecasts are
“correct”; it only requires the aggregate hit rate per bin. This is the
property that makes it suitable for Regime B evaluation.

### J.2 EDGap Definition

We adapt RES into a variance-weighted metric suitable for the ERM
setting. Let $`B_{1},\ldots,B_{K}`$ be confidence terciles (we use
$`K=3`$ throughout). Define:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\text{EDGap}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{n_{k}}{N}\cdot\frac{(\bar{o}_{k}-\bar{o})^{2}}{\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{2}+\epsilon}
``` |  | (5) |

where $`\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{2}=\bar{o}_{k}(1-\bar{o}_{k})/n_{k}`$ is the
estimated variance of $`\bar{o}_{k}`$ (Wilson form \[[Wilson,
1927](#bib.bib49)\]) and $`\epsilon>0`$ is a smoothing constant
preventing division by zero. The variance weighting downweights bins
with small sample size or extreme base rates, producing a more robust
signal than raw resolution.

### J.3 Properties

##### Non-negativity.

EDGap $`\geq 0`$ by construction (sum of squared terms divided by
positive denominators). EDGap $`=0`$ if and only if
$`\bar{o}_{k}=\bar{o}`$ for all $`k`$; the agent’s confidence carries no
discriminative information.

##### Monotonicity in discrimination.

If the agent’s confidence ranking is refined (more correct items in
higher-confidence bins), EDGap strictly increases. This follows because
the numerator $`(\bar{o}_{k}-\bar{o})^{2}`$ increases when bin hit rates
diverge from the base rate.

##### Independence from calibration.

EDGap measures *discrimination* (whether confidence separates correct
from incorrect), not *calibration* (whether
$`\bar{p}_{k}\approx\bar{o}_{k}`$). A perfectly calibrated but
non-discriminating agent (uniform confidence) achieves EDGap $`=0`$. A
poorly calibrated but highly discriminating agent (confidence inversely
correlated with correctness) achieves high EDGap. This independence is
by design: the separation theorem predicts that the epistemic axis
carries signal *regardless* of whether the agent’s confidence is
well-calibrated.

##### Computability in Regime B.

EDGap requires only the (confidence, binary outcome) sequence. In the
RLER factorial, “confidence” is the agent’s self-assessed reasoning
quality score, and “outcome” is whether the terminal answer is correct
(in Regime A) or whether the Judge’s structural assessment is positive
(in Regime B). The prequential calibration framework \[[Dawid,
1984](#bib.bib12), [Foster and Vohra, 1998](#bib.bib15)\] guarantees
that the Regime B approximation converges to the Regime A value as the
Judge’s prequential calibration error approaches zero.

### J.4 $`\Delta`$EDGap and the Separation Test

The separation claim rests on the *difference*
$`\Delta\text{EDGap}=\text{EDGap}(\textsc{rler})-\text{EDGap}(\textsc{rler\_Bad})`$
between well-specified and mis-specified protocols. Positive
$`\Delta`$EDGap means the well-specified protocol achieves better
epistemic discrimination. The statistical test uses case-level bootstrap
(10,000 trials): for each trial, resample the 132 cases with
replacement, recompute EDGap for both protocols, and record
$`\Delta`$EDGap. The 95% CI is the 2.5th–97.5th percentile of the
bootstrap distribution. If the CI excludes zero, the separation is
statistically significant at $`\alpha=0.05`$. The threshold-free forest
plot
(Figure [11](#A16.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ P.4 ΔEDGap Forest Plot ‣ Appendix P RLER White-Box Analysis ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward"))
visualizes these CIs across all factorial cells.

## Appendix K Cross-Benchmark Validation: CLadder $`\mathcal{L}_{2}`$

To validate that Rung Collapse is not an artifact of the CausalT5K
benchmark design, we examine evidence from CLadder \[[Jin et al.,
2023](#bib.bib18)\], an independently constructed causal reasoning
benchmark that systematically probes each rung of Pearl’s hierarchy
using symbolic questions derived from SCMs. CLadder provides a genuine
out-of-distribution test: its causal graphs, query templates, and
construction methodology are entirely independent of CausalT5K.

##### Published CLadder results.

Table [10](#A11.T10 "Table 10 ‣ Published CLadder results. ‣ Appendix K Cross-Benchmark Validation: CLadder ℒ_2 ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward")
reproduces the $`\mathcal{L}_{2}`$ (interventional) accuracy from [Jin
et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib18). The pattern is consistent with our
findings on CausalT5K: (i) accuracy degrades from $`\mathcal{L}_{1}`$ to
$`\mathcal{L}_{2}`$ (GPT-4 drops from 63.0% to 62.8% without prompting
aids, and from 83.4% to 67.5% with CausalCoT); (ii) even with
chain-of-thought prompting, $`\mathcal{L}_{2}`$ accuracy remains well
below $`\mathcal{L}_{1}`$; (iii) weaker models (GPT-3.5, LLaMA) perform
near chance on all rungs. The rung-dependent performance collapse across
an independent benchmark corroborates the structural nature of Rung
Collapse.

| Model | $`\mathcal{L}_{1}`$ | $`\mathcal{L}_{2}`$ | $`\mathcal{L}_{3}`$ |
|----|----|----|----|
| GPT-3.5 | 52.2% overall |  |  |
| GPT-4 (vanilla) | 63.0% | 62.8% | 60.6% |
| GPT-4 + CausalCoT | 83.4% | 67.5% | 62.1% |

Table 10: CLadder accuracy by rung (from [Jin et al.
\[2023\]](#bib.bib18), Table 2). Performance degrades from
$`\mathcal{L}_{1}`$ to $`\mathcal{L}_{2}`$/$`\mathcal{L}_{3}`$,
consistent with Rung Collapse on CausalT5K.

##### Partial replication with current models.

We additionally evaluate GPT-3.5-Turbo, Llama 3.3 70B^(‡), GPT-4-Turbo,
Claude Sonnet 4.5, and GPT-5.4-nano on CLadder’s $`\mathcal{L}_{2}`$
queries using the same zero-shot protocol as our CausalT5K experiments
(Appendix [L](#A12 "Appendix L Reproducibility and Experimental Setup ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward")),
with greedy decoding (temperature=0).
Table [11](#A11.T11 "Table 11 ‣ Partial replication with current models. ‣ Appendix K Cross-Benchmark Validation: CLadder ℒ_2 ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward")
reports collapse rates with Wilson 95% CIs on a 500-case label-balanced
subsample (250 YES + 250 NO).

| Model             | $`N`$ | Collapse $`\downarrow`$ | Accuracy $`\uparrow`$ |
|-------------------|-------|-------------------------|-----------------------|
| GPT-5.4-nano      | 500   | $`47.6\%\pm 4.4\%`$     | $`52.4\%\pm 4.4\%`$   |
| GPT-3.5 Turbo     | 500   | $`46.2\%\pm 4.3\%`$     | $`53.8\%\pm 4.3\%`$   |
| Llama 3.3 70B^(‡) | 500   | $`44.2\%\pm 4.3\%`$     | $`55.8\%\pm 4.3\%`$   |
| GPT-4 Turbo       | 500   | $`37.7\%\pm 4.3\%`$     | $`62.3\%\pm 4.2\%`$   |
| Claude Sonnet 4.5 | 500   | $`31.1\%\pm 4.0\%`$     | $`68.9\%\pm 4.0\%`$   |

Table 11: Rung Collapse rates on CLadder $`\mathcal{L}_{2}`$ (500-case
subsample, zero-shot, temperature=0). Wilson 95% CIs. Cross-benchmark
validation of
Table [1](#S3.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 3.1 RQ1: Detection, Scaling, and Correction ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward").

##### Interpretation.

Our GPT replication closely matches the published CLadder numbers
(GPT-3.5: 52.2%, GPT-4: 62.0% overall accuracy in [Jin et al.
\[2023\]](#bib.bib18); our L2-only subsample: 53.8% and 62.3%). Across
all five models and three provider families (OpenAI, Meta/Groq,
Anthropic), Rung Collapse rates range from 31.1% (Claude Sonnet 4.5) to
47.6% (GPT-5.4-nano), with Llama 3.3 70B at 44.2%; this confirms that
the phenomenon is not vendor-specific and affects open-weight models
comparably. GPT-5.4-nano collapses *more* than GPT-3.5 (47.6% vs.
46.2%), suggesting that the “nano” distillation trades causal reasoning
depth for latency; this finding is consistent with the Reward
Entrenchment mechanism
(Definition [2](#Thmdefinition2 "Definition 2 (Reward Entrenchment). ‣ 2.1 Background: Rung Collapse and Reward Entrenchment ‣ 2 Epistemic Regret Minimization ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward")),
where outcome-optimized distillation reinforces associational shortcuts.
The CausalT5K collapse rates are lower overall (e.g. 17.3% and 12.5% for
GPT-3.5 and GPT-4) because CausalT5K uses natural-language scenarios
where domain knowledge provides partial signal, whereas CLadder uses
purely symbolic causal graphs that strip away all associational
shortcuts. The *direction* of the scaling effect is identical across
both benchmarks, supporting the claim that Rung Collapse is a structural
property of autoregressive training rather than a benchmark-specific
artifact.

![Refer to caption](2602.11675v4/figures/cross_benchmark_comparison.png)

Figure 5: Cross-benchmark validation: CausalT5K vs. CLadder. For the
three models tested on both benchmarks, Rung Collapse is
$`{\sim}3\times`$ higher on CLadder (symbolic graphs strip domain
shortcuts), but the *scaling direction* is identical: collapse decreases
with model capability on both benchmarks. Ratios shown above each pair.

![Refer to caption](2602.11675v4/figures/cladder_scatter_5models.png)

Figure 6: CLadder $`\mathcal{L}_{2}`$: Collapse vs. Accuracy. Five
models from three providers. The “distillation penalty” anomaly:
GPT-5.4-nano collapses more than GPT-3.5 despite being a newer model,
consistent with the Reward Entrenchment mechanism
(Definition [2](#Thmdefinition2 "Definition 2 (Reward Entrenchment). ‣ 2.1 Background: Rung Collapse and Reward Entrenchment ‣ 2 Epistemic Regret Minimization ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward"))
applied to distillation. Dashed line:
$`\text{collapse}+\text{accuracy}=100\%`$. Wilson 95% CIs on both axes.

## Appendix L Reproducibility and Experimental Setup

### L.1 Model Specifications

All ERM experiments
(Section [3](#S3 "3 Experiments ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward"))
were conducted via official APIs between January and February 2026 using
greedy decoding (temperature=0.0). The Exp 2b label-free ablation
(Appendix [P.7](#A16.SS7 "P.7 Label-Free Single-Episode Ablation (Exp 2b) ‣ Appendix P RLER White-Box Analysis ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward"))
was conducted in April 2026 using the same model endpoint; absolute
recovery rates differ from the February run due to API-side model
updates, but within-run comparisons remain valid. Model versions:
GPT-3.5 Turbo: gpt-3.5-turbo-0125; GPT-4 Turbo: gpt-4-turbo-2024-04-09;
GPT-5.2: gpt-5.2-2025-12-11 (using max_completion_tokens=1000 to
accommodate extended reasoning output); Gemini 2.5 Flash:
gemini-2.5-flash; Claude Sonnet 3.5: claude-3-5-sonnet-20241022; Llama
3.3 70B: llama-3.3-70b-versatile (served via Groq). Total computational
budget: approximately \$200 USD.

### L.2 Prompt Templates

##### Experiment A (Detection).

Zero-shot template: *“Scenario: {scenario_text}. Claim: {causal_claim}.
Question: Is the claim causally valid? Answer (YES/NO) first, then
explain.”*

##### Experiment B (Correction).

Standard Correction (Control): *“You previously analyzed a causal
scenario. Your Previous Answer: {model_original_response}. CRITIQUE: Are
you sure this is correct? Please re-evaluate the causal link critically.
Answer (YES/NO) first, then explain.”* ERM Correction (Treatment): *“You
previously evaluated a claim incorrectly. CRITIQUE FROM SENIOR AUDITOR:
‘{wise_refusal_text}’. Task: Re-evaluate the claim given this critique.
Answer (YES/NO) first, then explain.”*

Note on error-direction cues. The single-episode Exp B prompt includes
“you previously evaluated a claim incorrectly,” which is an
error-direction cue derived from knowing the gold label. The critique
text itself (wise_refusal_text) is a pre-written causal rationale from
the benchmark, not generated by a label-free judge. This design tests
ERM’s correction mechanism under favorable conditions. Three controls
isolate the contributions of each component: (i) Exp 2b
(Appendix [P.7](#A16.SS7 "P.7 Label-Free Single-Episode Ablation (Exp 2b) ‣ Appendix P RLER White-Box Analysis ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward"))
ablates the error-direction cue in a six-condition factorial on both
stubborn models (GPT-4T $`N{=}170`$, GPT-5.2 $`N{\approx}1{,}350`$;
$`n{\approx}49`$ false-flip tests per condition each). Label-free
critique without the cue (C4, C5) achieves 0% false-flips for both
models (Fisher $`p<10^{-5}`$). For GPT-4T, causal vocabulary is
load-bearing even under label-free generation ($`+21.4`$ pp,
$`p=0.0001`$). (ii) The cross-episode protocol
(§[3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 RQ2: Cross-Episode Signal ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward"))
removes all error-direction cues and generates critiques from a
label-free judge. (iii) The matched-richness ablation
(§[3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 RQ1: Detection, Scaling, and Correction ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward"))
controls for prompt richness. Together, these establish that ERM-style
critique is both effective and safe when deployed without the
error-direction cue.

### L.3 CausalT5K Benchmark Justification

We chose CausalT5K over standard reasoning benchmarks for three reasons:
(i) *ladder level precision*: every case is explicitly
$`\mathcal{L}_{2}`$-hard, with a strong $`\mathcal{L}_{1}`$ signal that
must be rejected; (ii) *trap diversity*: 18 specific trap types isolate
structural failures rather than simple logic puzzles; (iii)
*contamination resistance*: scenarios are procedurally generated from
structural causal models, reducing memorization effects.

##### Benchmark adjudication and label-noise bound.

The recovery analysis reveals that some hard-to-correct cases may
reflect benchmark limitations rather than ERM failures. Three cases
(2.34, 2.35, 2.112) are flagged for formal adjudication: when four or
more frontier LLMs provide strong interventional reasoning for a claim
that the ground truth rejects, and the wise-refusal text contains
evidence supporting the claim it denies, the label warrants review. We
recommend a three-annotator panel with a “contested” label for cases
where reasonable experts disagree. To bound the effect on reported
metrics: 3 of 1,360 cases yields a *lower bound* label-noise rate of
$`0.22\%`$. If we conservatively assume that undetected label noise
follows the same rate as detected noise (i.e., cases where *fewer* than
4 models disagree may also have ambiguous labels), an upper bound of
$`{\sim}1`$–$`2\%`$ is plausible. At 2% label noise, the Rung Collapse
rate for GPT-5.2 (3.7%) could be inflated to at most $`3.7+2.0=5.7\%`$
or deflated to $`1.7\%`$; the qualitative conclusion (scaling reduces
but does not eliminate collapse) is robust. For ERM correction rates,
the 3 flagged cases fall in the “hard to correct” category and do not
affect the qualitative conclusion.

## Appendix M Baseline Suite: ERM vs. Standard Reasoning Baselines

To address the concern that ERM is compared only against outcome-only or
internally designed baselines, we evaluate six correction methods on the
same GPT-4 Turbo Wolf Cases ($`N{=}170`$) from Exp A, all operating
under the gold-free protocol (no error-direction cue, no gold labels).
We also measure false-flip rates on 50 randomly sampled correct cases.
In addition to recovery rate (accuracy) and false-flip rate, we report
*residual Rung Collapse*: whether the corrected trace still substitutes
$`\mathcal{L}_{1}`$ reasoning for the $`\mathcal{L}_{2}`$ query,
assessed by a GPT-4o judge.

| Method | Recovery | Collapse | False-flip | Calls | $`\boldsymbol{p}`$ vs ERM |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| Outcome-only reprompt | $`52.9\%`$ | $`26.5\%`$ | $`8.2\%`$ | 1 | $`.013`$ |
| Self-consistency $`N{=}3`$ \[[Wang et al., 2023](#bib.bib47)\] | $`30.8\%`$ | $`63.3\%`$ | $`0.0\%`$ | 3 | $`{<}.0001`$ |
| Best-of-$`N`$ confidence \[[Snell et al., 2024](#bib.bib45)\] | $`15.9\%`$ | $`70.0\%`$ | $`2.0\%`$ | 3 | $`{<}.0001`$ |
| Self-Refine \[[Madaan et al., 2023](#bib.bib51)\] | $`14.8\%`$ | $`55.0\%`$ | $`2.0\%`$ | 2 | $`{<}.0001`$ |
| Matched-richness non-causal | $`37.9\%`$ | $`32.0\%`$ | $`2.0\%`$ | 2 | $`{<}.0001`$ |
| ERM gold-free causal | $`\mathbf{64.1\%}`$ | $`\mathbf{4.1\%}`$ | $`\mathbf{0.0\%}`$ | 2 | — |

Table 12: Baseline suite on GPT-4 Turbo Wolf Cases ($`N{=}170`$).
Recovery = fraction of Wolf Cases corrected. Rung Collapse = fraction of
corrected traces still exhibiting $`\mathcal{L}_{1}`$ substitution.
False-flip measured on 50 correct cases. McNemar $`p`$ vs. ERM.

Three findings emerge. *First*, ERM dominates on both recovery and Rung
Collapse reduction: 64.1% recovery with only 4.1% residual Rung
Collapse, versus 30.8% / 63.3% for self-consistency and 14.8% / 55.0%
for Self-Refine. All pairwise McNemar tests are significant
($`p<0.0001`$). *Second*, self-consistency and Best-of-$`N`$
*underperform* outcome-only reprompting (30.8% and 15.9% vs. 52.9%),
directly confirming the paper’s argument that majority voting reinforces
$`\mathcal{L}_{1}`$ shortcuts on causal tasks: multiple incorrect chains
converge on the same associational answer
(Corollary [1](#Thmcorollary1 "Corollary 1 (LLM Rung Collapse: immediate from CHT). ‣ 2.1 Background: Rung Collapse and Reward Entrenchment ‣ 2 Epistemic Regret Minimization ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward")).
Best-of-$`N`$ confidence selection is worst because the model is most
confident when it has the strongest $`\mathcal{L}_{1}`$ justification,
which is precisely wrong on $`\mathcal{L}_{2}`$ tasks — a direct
manifestation of Reward Entrenchment. *Third*, ERM achieves 0%
false-flips (matching self-consistency) while all other critique-based
methods introduce some false-flip risk (2.0–8.2%).

These results establish that ERM’s advantage is not merely relative to a
weak outcome-only baseline; it outperforms every standard test-time
reasoning-improvement method on the same task, and uniquely reduces Rung
Collapse to near-zero levels.

##### Model scope.

We run the full baseline suite on GPT-4 Turbo because it provides the
larger stable Wolf set ($`N{=}170`$). GPT-5.2 is already covered by the
matched-richness ablation
(§[3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 RQ1: Detection, Scaling, and Correction ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward"))
and the label-free factorial
(Appendix [P.7](#A16.SS7 "P.7 Label-Free Single-Episode Ablation (Exp 2b) ‣ Appendix P RLER White-Box Analysis ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward")),
where the same qualitative pattern holds: structured causal critique,
not outcome-only reprompting, supplies the corrective signal.

## Appendix N Cross-Model Stubborn-Case Taxonomy

Our five-model study on CausalL2 identifies 51 cases where *all* models
failed to recover from initial errors despite structural critique.
Table [13](#A14.T13 "Table 13 ‣ Appendix N Cross-Model Stubborn-Case Taxonomy ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward")
categorizes these universally stubborn cases by failure mode.

| Failure Mode                                    | Count | %     |
|-------------------------------------------------|-------|-------|
| Confounding (failed to identify backdoor paths) | 17    | 33.3% |
| Rung Collapse (L1 evidence for L2 claims)       | 16    | 31.4% |
| Empty Verification (label with no trace)        | 8     | 15.7% |
| Other (RTM, Post-Hoc, Overgeneralization)       | 5     | 9.8%  |
| Collider / Selection Bias                       | 3     | 5.9%  |
| Measurement Error                               | 2     | 3.9%  |
| Total                                           | 51    | 100%  |

Table 13: Distribution of universally stubborn cases by failure mode
($`n{=}51`$, all five models failed).

Three patterns dominate. *Confounding* (33.3%): models correctly state
that “other factors may explain the association” but fail to name the
specific confounder or draw the backdoor path—a declarative-procedural
gap where the knowledge exists but cannot be operationalized. *Rung
Collapse* (31.4%): models cite “historical trends” or “correlations” (L1
evidence) to support interventional claims (L2), even when the vignette
describes a randomized trial. *Empty Verification* (15.7%): models
output “VALID” with no supporting trace, bypassing deliberation
entirely—a regime where process verification has no signal to evaluate.

This taxonomy validates ERM’s failure-mode registry
(Table [6](#A5.T6 "Table 6 ‣ Appendix E Algorithm Details ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward"))
on an independent dataset and confirms that the bifurcation between
correctable and stubborn cases is consistent across model families.

## Appendix O ERM White-Box Analysis: Stubborn Cases

Of the 1,360 CausalT5K cases, 42 defeated four or more models (“stubborn
cases”) and 6 defeated all six. We call these *universal failures*.
Table [14](#A15.T14 "Table 14 ‣ Appendix O ERM White-Box Analysis: Stubborn Cases ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward")
summarizes the distribution.

| Severity        | Count | History | Other | Surviving Models   |
|-----------------|-------|---------|-------|--------------------|
| 6/6 (Universal) | 6     | 4       | 2     | None               |
| 5/6             | 21    | 17      | 4     | Sonnet 3.5 (18/21) |
| 4/6             | 15    | 12      | 3     | Various            |
| Total           | 42    | 33      | 9     |                    |

Table 14: Stubborn cases ($`\geq`$4/6 models failed) by severity.

### O.1 The Six Universal Failures

Table [15](#A15.T15 "Table 15 ‣ O.1 The Six Universal Failures ‣ Appendix O ERM White-Box Analysis: Stubborn Cases ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward")
lists all cases where every model committed Rung Collapse. They cluster
into two failure archetypes.

| Case  | Domain     | Trap Type                    | Arch. |
|-------|------------|------------------------------|-------|
| 2.095 | Daily Life | T5 (Regression to Mean)      | I     |
| 2.087 | Daily Life | T5 (Regression to Mean)      | I     |
| F.129 | History    | T4 (Survivorship Bias)       | II    |
| 2.34  | History    | T17 (Mechanism Conflation)   | II    |
| 2.35  | History    | T15 (Information Distortion) | II    |
| 2.112 | History    | T7 (Confounding)             | II    |

Table 15: The six universal failures. Cases are grouped by archetype: I
= Meta-Validation, II = Compelling Mechanism.

##### Archetype 1: The Meta-Validation Trap.

Cases 2.095 and 2.087 share a distinctive structure: each presents a
character who *correctly identifies* regression to the mean (RTM) as a
threat to causal inference. All six models validated the character’s
reasoning and answered YES. The ground truth is NO: the character’s
identification of RTM is correct, but the causal claim (that RTM
explains the improvement) cannot be established without a control group.
The models conflate *recognizing* a valid statistical concept with
*establishing* it as the causal mechanism, a second-order Rung Collapse
where the models perform correct $`\mathcal{L}_{1}`$ reasoning about the
RTM phenomenon but fail the $`\mathcal{L}_{2}`$ question of whether RTM
is the actual mechanism.

##### Archetype 2: The Compelling Mechanism.

The remaining four universal failures (all History) present causal
mechanisms so strongly supported by common knowledge that all models
accept monocausal attribution, missing the confounders specified in the
ground truth. In Case F.129 (Weimar hyperinflation), all six models
provided detailed, historically accurate accounts of deficit
monetization. The wise refusal identifies Survivorship Bias: the
monetary-policy narrative is constructed from data about the government
that *survived* the reparations crisis, not from a comparison with
counterfactual policy paths. The models’ reasoning is not wrong; it is
incomplete. The mechanism is real, but the causal attribution is invalid
without accounting for selection effects.

### O.2 Domain Asymmetry: Why History Is Hard

![Refer to caption](2602.11675v4/figures/domain_asymmetry_stubborn.png)

Figure 7: Left: Stubborn cases ($`\geq`$4/6 models failed) by domain.
History accounts for 79% of stubborn cases despite comprising only 20%
of scenarios. Right: Recovery rates for the two universal failure
archetypes. Compelling Mechanism cases (History) show high variance
(33–100%), while Meta-Validation cases cluster at 50–83%.

History accounts for 33 of the 42 stubborn cases (79%), despite
comprising only one of five domains. Three properties of historical
reasoning explain this concentration: (i) *counterfactual
inaccessibility*: historical events are one-shot, so the “what would
have happened otherwise?” question cannot be answered empirically; (ii)
*narrative coherence bias*: historical writing privileges monocausal
arcs over multi-causal complexity, and the training corpus inherits this
conflation; and (iii) *training distribution alignment*: the
autoregressive corpus contains abundant text asserting historical
monocausal claims as authoritative, creating a strong
$`\mathcal{L}_{1}`$ prior that no $`\mathcal{L}_{2}`$ corrective exists
in the training data to counterbalance.

### O.3 The Claude Survival Pattern

Among the 21 cases where exactly 5/6 models failed, Claude Sonnet 3.5
was the sole survivor in 18. Comparing Claude’s responses against
GPT-5.2’s reveals a consistent qualitative difference along two
dimensions. *Systematic doubt*: Claude’s correct responses begin by
acknowledging the plausibility of the mechanism, then enumerate specific
missing causal conditions (e.g., on automobiles and suburbanization,
Claude listed temporal issues, confounders, and missing counterfactuals
where GPT-5.2 wrote a confident YES). *Explicit causal vocabulary*:
Claude deploys terms like “selection bias,” “confounding bias,” and
“temporal correlation is not causation” as active audit criteria rather
than rhetorical hedges. This behavioral pattern is consistent with an
alignment objective that penalizes unsupported causal claims,
effectively implementing a soft ConfounderBlind guard
(Table [6](#A5.T6 "Table 6 ‣ Appendix E Algorithm Details ‣ Epistemic Regret Minimization: Label-Free Causal Critique Beyond Outcome Reward"))
natively. The implication for ERM is direct: if Claude’s advantage stems
from an internalized guard, then ERM’s explicit guard injection should
confer a similar advantage on models that lack it, confirmed by the
significant correction gains for GPT-4 Turbo and GPT-5.2 in
Experiment B.

### O.4 Implications for ERM

The stubborn cases yield specific predictions, all confirmed in
Experiment B: (i) Meta-Validation cases (Archetype I) are correctable by
ERM, because the epistemic signal can point out that identifying RTM is
not equivalent to establishing it as the cause, a distinction the models
have the vocabulary to express; (ii) Compelling Mechanism cases
(Archetype II) are also correctable (F.129 achieves 100% recovery once
the bias is named), though cases with ambiguous ground-truth labels
(2.34, 2.35, 2.112) show lower recovery, suggesting benchmark
limitations rather than ERM failures; (iii) the ConfounderBlind guard is
particularly effective because it operationalizes the causal skepticism
that distinguishes Claude’s correct responses from other models’
failures.

in this subsample (at $`N{=}466`$, $`\Delta\text{Acc}`$ reaches
````
